	\section*{Evaluated criteria}
	\begin{enumerate}[label=\textbf{C\arabic*}]
		\item Characterises decision-making problems under uncertainty by identifying decision alternatives, states of nature, consequences, and the economic or financial context of the decision.
		\item[\textbf{C3}] Calculates profit as revenue minus costs across states of nature and organises the resulting payoffs in payoff tables.
	\end{enumerate}

\clearpage
\subsection{From a decision situation to a profit table}
\label{subsec:intro-first-steps}

\subsubsection*{Start with the situation}
A school enterprise organises services for community events. Before bookings
are confirmed, its students must choose either a basic service plan or an
expanded service plan with more equipment and capacity. Demand may turn out
to be high or low. The enterprise chooses its plan first; demand is revealed
later. This timing makes the decision uncertain.

The uncertain process is customers making bookings. High demand and low
demand are the two possible \textbf{states} of that process in this model.
The students control the service plan, but they cannot choose the demand state.
A consequence can first be described in words: ``the basic plan operates under
high demand, earns sales income, and uses staff and supplies.'' We then assign
a monetary \GTenToneHL{payoff}{payoff} to that consequence. Here the \GTenToneHL{payoff}{payoff} will be the \GTenToneHL{payoff}{profit}
remaining after \GTenToneHL{cost}{costs}. Describing what happens and measuring its financial
result are related, but different, tasks.

\subsubsection*{Name the choices, then the states}
The basic and expanded plans are the \textbf{\GTenToneHL{alternative}{alternatives}}: the available
choices. We now shorten their names to \(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1}\), read ``\GTenToneHL{alternative}{alternative} one,''
and \(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_2}\), read ``\GTenToneHL{alternative}{alternative} two.''
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule Symbol & Choice made by the enterprise \\ \midrule
\(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1}\) & Choose the basic service plan. \\
\(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_2}\) & Choose the expanded service plan. \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
High and low demand are possible outcomes outside the enterprise's control.
We label them \(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1}\), read ``state one,'' and \(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_2}\), read ``state two.''
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule Symbol & Demand outcome revealed after the choice \\ \midrule
\(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1}\) & High demand. \\
\(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_2}\) & Low demand. \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The small numbers below the letters are \textbf{subscripts}. They identify
choices and states; they do not rank them or give their monetary values.

\textbf{Check.} Can the enterprise choose \(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1}\)?
\textit{Suggested answer:} No. It chooses a plan; customers' bookings determine
which demand state occurs.

\clearpage
\subsubsection*{Match one choice with one state}
To describe a possible result, name both the chosen plan and the demand
outcome. The pair \((\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1})\), read ``\GTenToneHL{alternative}{alternative} one with state one,''
means the basic plan under high demand. There are four combinations:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule Pair & Contextual meaning \\ \midrule
\((\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1})\) & Basic plan, high demand. \\
\((\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_2})\) & Basic plan, low demand. \\
\((\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_2},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1})\) & Expanded plan, high demand. \\
\((\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_2},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_2})\) & Expanded plan, low demand. \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Only one pair will be realised for this particular decision. We analyse all
four beforehand so that the financial consequence of each possible
choice--state combination is recorded.

\subsubsection*{Give the consequence a monetary value}
\textbf{\GTenToneHL{revenue}{Revenue}} is money earned from the activity. \textbf{\GTenToneHL{cost}{Cost}} is money
spent or resources charged to the activity. \textbf{Profit} is \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue} minus
\GTenToneHL{cost}{cost}. Suppose the basic plan under high demand earns 20 thousand USD and
\GTenToneHL{cost}{costs} 9 thousand USD. Its \GTenToneHL{payoff}{profit} is \(20-9=11\) thousand USD.
One thousand USD means 1,000 US dollars. Throughout these introductory
examples, monetary totals are in thousand USD. \GTenToneHL{revenue}{Revenue} alone is not the
\GTenToneHL{payoff}{payoff} in this activity: \GTenToneHL{payoff}{profit} is.

We introduce a separate symbol for each total. First,
\[
\GTenToneHL{revenue}{R}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1})=20.
\]
Read this as ``total \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue} for \GTenToneHL{alternative}{alternative} one in state one is 20 thousand
USD.'' The capital \(R\) names total \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue}; \(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1}\) identifies the basic
plan and \(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1}\) identifies high demand. The parentheses identify the inputs
to this \GTenToneHL{quantity}{quantity}; they do not mean multiplication.

Next, write the corresponding total \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} as
\[
\GTenToneHL{cost}{C}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1})=9.
\]
Read this as ``total \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} for \GTenToneHL{alternative}{alternative} one in state one is 9 thousand
USD.'' Capital \(C\) names total \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost}, with the same two inputs.

Finally, capital \(P\) names \GTenToneHL{payoff}{profit}, which is our \GTenToneHL{payoff}{payoff}:
\[
\GTenToneHL{payoff}{P}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1})=\GTenToneHL{revenue}{R}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1})-\GTenToneHL{cost}{C}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1})=11.
\]
Read this as ``\GTenToneHL{payoff}{profit} for \GTenToneHL{alternative}{alternative} one in state one equals its \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue}
minus its \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost}: 11 thousand USD.'' All three quantities refer to the
\emph{same} plan and demand state. The result is positive because \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue}
exceeds \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost}. In separate small illustrations, \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue} 9 and \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} 9 give
zero \GTenToneHL{payoff}{profit} (\(9-9=0\)); \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue} 8 and \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} 9 give a loss
(\(8-9=-1\) thousand USD).

\subsubsection*{Organise the four profits}
A \GTenToneHL{payoff}{profit} \GTenToneHL{payoff}{payoff} table puts \GTenToneHL{alternative}{alternatives} in rows and states in columns.
For example, take the basic plan's high-demand \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue} of 20 and matching
\GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} of 9, subtract to obtain 11, then put 11 in row \(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1}\), column
\(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1}\). Repeat that process for the other three pairs. The next problem
consolidates this process using directly supplied totals. Its input table
records the data; the paired calculation tables match each symbolic
subtraction to its arithmetic; the clean final table contains profits only.

\textbf{Check.} Could we subtract the \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} for \((\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_2},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1})\) from the \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue}
for \((\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1})\)? \textit{Suggested answer:} No. That would mix two plans.
Both amounts in a subtraction must belong to the same \GTenToneHL{alternative}{alternative}--state pair.

\subsection{Introductory General Problem (C1--C3)}
	\label{sec:problem1-introductory-general-c1-c3}

	Use the school service enterprise just described. It must commit to either \GTenToneHL{alternative}{alternative} \(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1}\) or \GTenToneHL{alternative}{alternative} \(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_2}\) before learning whether demand will be high (\(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1}\)) or low (\(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_2}\)). Its accounting team used sales forecasts for each choice under each demand outcome to estimate total \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue}, and used purchasing and operating budgets to estimate the corresponding total \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost}. All amounts and the resulting \GTenToneHL{payoff}{profit} are measured in thousand USD.

	If the enterprise selects \(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1}\), forecast sales produce total \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue} of \(20\) under demand state \(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1}\) and \(13\) under \(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_2}\); fulfilling those sales is budgeted to \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} \(9\) and \(8\), respectively. If it selects \(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_2}\), its different sales plan is expected to bring in \(18\) under \(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1}\) and \(15\) under \(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_2}\), while the resources required by that plan \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} \(10\) and \(9\), respectively. The enterprise must calculate the \GTenToneHL{payoff}{profit} for every possible \GTenToneHL{alternative}{alternative}--state pair before constructing its \GTenToneHL{payoff}{payoff} table.

	\textbf{Questions/Tasks.}
	\begin{enumerate}
		\CriterionTask
		{subsec:problem1-introductory-general-c1}{1}
		{Interpret the decision elements.}

		\CriterionTask
		{subsec:problem1-introductory-general-c3}{3}
		{Build the payoff table.}
	\end{enumerate}

	\subsubsection*{C1 - Interpreting the decision.}
	\phantomsection
	\label{subsec:problem1-introductory-general-c1}

	\DecisionElementsTable
	{Basic service plan; Expanded service plan}
	{High demand; Low demand}
	{Customer bookings reveal demand after the enterprise chooses its plan}
	{Services are delivered with sales income and resource use; \GTenToneHL{payoff}{profit} measures the monetary consequence in thousand USD}

	\secInicio[sec:problem1-introductory-general-c1-c3][sec:introduction]

	\clearpage
	\subsubsection*{C3 - Calculating revenue, cost, and payoff.}
	\phantomsection
	\label{subsec:problem1-introductory-general-c3}

\textbf{1. Symbols, values, and meanings.}

\begin{longtable}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.19\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.25\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.50\linewidth}@{}}
\toprule Symbol & Value & Meaning in this problem. \\ \midrule
\endhead
\(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1}\) & --- & Operating \GTenToneHL{alternative}{alternative} 1. \\
\(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_2}\) & --- & Operating \GTenToneHL{alternative}{alternative} 2. \\
\(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1}\) & --- & Demand outcome 1. \\
\(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_2}\) & --- & Demand outcome 2. \\
\(\GTenToneHL{revenue}{R}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1})\) & 20 thousand USD & Forecast total \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue} for \(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1}\) under \(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1}\). \\
\(\GTenToneHL{cost}{C}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1})\) & 9 thousand USD & Budgeted total \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} for \(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1}\) under \(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1}\). \\
\(\GTenToneHL{revenue}{R}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_2})\) & 13 thousand USD & Forecast total \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue} for \(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1}\) under \(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_2}\). \\
\(\GTenToneHL{cost}{C}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_2})\) & 8 thousand USD & Budgeted total \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} for \(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1}\) under \(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_2}\). \\
\(\GTenToneHL{revenue}{R}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_2},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1})\) & 18 thousand USD & Forecast total \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue} for \(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_2}\) under \(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1}\). \\
\(\GTenToneHL{cost}{C}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_2},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1})\) & 10 thousand USD & Budgeted total \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} for \(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_2}\) under \(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1}\). \\
\(\GTenToneHL{revenue}{R}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_2},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_2})\) & 15 thousand USD & Forecast total \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue} for \(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_2}\) under \(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_2}\). \\
\(\GTenToneHL{cost}{C}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_2},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_2})\) & 9 thousand USD & Budgeted total \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} for \(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_2}\) under \(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_2}\). \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}

\textbf{2. Symbolic and numerical calculations.}

All calculated amounts below are in thousand USD. The row and column indices refer to the input table.

\CalculationPair{Profit}{
\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}
\toprule
Alternative & \(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1}\) & \(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_2}\) \\
\midrule
\(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1}\) & \SymbolicCell{\GTenToneHL{revenue}{R}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1})-\GTenToneHL{cost}{C}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1})}{\GTenToneHL{payoff}{P}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1})} & \SymbolicCell{\GTenToneHL{revenue}{R}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_2})-\GTenToneHL{cost}{C}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_2})}{\GTenToneHL{payoff}{P}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_2})} \\
\(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_2}\) & \SymbolicCell{\GTenToneHL{revenue}{R}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_2},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1})-\GTenToneHL{cost}{C}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_2},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1})}{\GTenToneHL{payoff}{P}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_2},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1})} & \SymbolicCell{\GTenToneHL{revenue}{R}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_2},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_2})-\GTenToneHL{cost}{C}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_2},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_2})}{\GTenToneHL{payoff}{P}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_2},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_2})} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}}{
\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}
\toprule
Alternative & \(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1}\) & \(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_2}\) \\

\(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_2}\) & \CalcResult{\GTenToneHL{revenue}{18}-\GTenToneHL{cost}{10}}{\GTenToneHL{payoff}{8}} & \CalcResult{\GTenToneHL{revenue}{15}-\GTenToneHL{cost}{9}}{\GTenToneHL{payoff}{6}} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}}

\subsection{Build totals from quantities, rates, and fixed amounts}
\label{subsec:intro-components}

\subsubsection*{Generalise a familiar calculation}
The first problem has two choices and two states. To refer to any one of
them without writing a separate rule for every cell, use \(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i}\), read
``\GTenToneHL{alternative}{alternative} i,'' and \(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j}\), read ``state j.'' The letters \(i\) and
\(j\) are \textbf{indices}: they tell us which \GTenToneHL{alternative}{alternative} and which state
to use. For example, substituting \(i=2\) and \(j=1\) gives \(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_2}\),
\(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1}\), and the expanded-plan, high-demand pair \((\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_2},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1})\).
In the first problem, \(\GTenToneHL{revenue}{R}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_2},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1})=\GTenToneHL{revenue}{18}\), \(\GTenToneHL{cost}{C}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_2},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1})=\GTenToneHL{cost}{10}\), and
\(\GTenToneHL{payoff}{P}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_2},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1})=\GTenToneHL{payoff}{18}-10=8\) thousand USD. The indices 2 and 1 are identifiers;
the number 8 is a monetary value. The number of \GTenToneHL{alternative}{alternatives} is a count,
not an index or a \GTenToneHL{payoff}{profit}. Later problems may have more choices or states.

The relationship already used in every cell can now be written generally:
\[
\GTenToneHL{payoff}{P}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})=\GTenToneHL{revenue}{R}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})-\GTenToneHL{cost}{C}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j}).
\]
This says ``\GTenToneHL{payoff}{profit} for a chosen \GTenToneHL{alternative}{alternative} and a particular state is the
\GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue} for that pair minus the \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} for that same pair.''

\subsubsection*{Why build revenue and cost?}
A \textbf{direct model}, such as the first problem, supplies total \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue}
and total \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost}. Use those totals as given; we cannot infer a unique set of
prices, quantities, or fixed payments from them. A \textbf{component model}
instead supplies ingredients: quantities, rates per unit, and fixed amounts.
We must use those ingredients to build total \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue} and total \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost}
\emph{independently}, then subtract.

We now study a new version of the same school service enterprise. Its plans
and demand labels keep their meanings, but its financial arrangements will
be stated component by component. These are new assumptions, not a
breakdown of the first problem's directly supplied totals. Each extension
below explicitly adds an arrangement to this new version.

\subsubsection*{Quantity: how much service is completed?}
Suppose the basic plan completes two service batches under high demand and
one under low demand. A \textbf{service batch} is one package of work for an
event; it is the \GTenToneHL{quantity}{quantity} unit throughout this running example. The expanded
plan can complete three batches under high demand and two under low demand.
Write \(\GTenToneHL{quantity}{q}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1})=\GTenToneHL{quantity}{2}\) batches, read ``\GTenToneHL{quantity}{quantity} for \GTenToneHL{alternative}{alternative} one in state
one is two batches.'' More generally, \(\GTenToneHL{quantity}{q}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})\) means the \GTenToneHL{quantity}{quantity} for
the named pair.
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}
\toprule Quantity (service batches) & \(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1}\): high demand & \(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_2}\): low demand \\ \midrule
\(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1}\): basic plan & \GTenToneHL{payoff}{2} & \GTenToneHL{payoff}{1} \\
\(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_2}\): expanded plan & \GTenToneHL{payoff}{3} & \GTenToneHL{payoff}{2} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Demand affects bookings, while the chosen plan affects capacity, so \GTenToneHL{quantity}{quantity}
can depend on both inputs. A \GTenToneHL{quantity}{quantity} of 2 batches is not 2 thousand USD.

\subsubsection*{A revenue rate determined by the plan}
Assume initially that the only \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue} is payment for each completed batch.
The basic plan earns 10 thousand USD per batch; the expanded plan earns 11
thousand USD per batch for its larger service package. These are earning
\textbf{rates}, rather than total amounts. Write \(\GTenToneHL{revenue}{r}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1})=\GTenToneHL{revenue}{10}\) and
\(\GTenToneHL{revenue}{r}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_2})=\GTenToneHL{revenue}{11}\) thousand USD per batch. Read \(\GTenToneHL{revenue}{r}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1})\) as ``\GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue} rate
for \GTenToneHL{alternative}{alternative} one.'' More generally, \(\GTenToneHL{revenue}{r}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i})\) is the rate set by the plan.

Multiply the number of batches by the payment for each batch:
\[
\GTenToneHL{revenue}{R}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})=\GTenToneHL{quantity}{q}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})\GTenToneHL{revenue}{r}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i}).
\]
For the basic plan under high demand, substitute the \GTenToneHL{quantity}{quantity} and rate:
\[
\GTenToneHL{revenue}{R}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1})=\begin{gathered}2\times10\\[2pt]=20\end{gathered}\quad\text{(thousand USD).}
\]
The units are batches times thousand USD per batch, giving thousand USD.
The basic plan's rate stays at 10 across its row; under low demand its
\GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue} is \(1\times10=10\) thousand USD because \GTenToneHL{quantity}{quantity} changes.
Dependence tells us which input determines a component. It does not require
different inputs to have different values: two plans could happen to have
equal rates.

\subsubsection*{A cost rate determined by the plan}
Now add a \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} assumption: each basic-plan batch uses 3 thousand USD of
resources, while each expanded-plan batch uses 4 thousand USD. These are
\GTenToneHL{cost}{costs} \emph{per batch}. Write \(\GTenToneHL{cost}{c}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1})=\GTenToneHL{cost}{3}\), read ``\GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} rate for \GTenToneHL{alternative}{alternative}
one,'' and \(\GTenToneHL{cost}{c}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_2})=\GTenToneHL{cost}{4}\), in thousand USD per batch. The general plan-based
\GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} rate is \(\GTenToneHL{cost}{c}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i})\). If these are the only \GTenToneHL{cost}{costs},
\[
\GTenToneHL{cost}{C}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})=\GTenToneHL{quantity}{q}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})\GTenToneHL{cost}{c}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i}).
\]
Keep the \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue} and \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} calculations separate before finding \GTenToneHL{payoff}{profit}:
\[
\begin{aligned}
\GTenToneHL{revenue}{R}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1})&=\begin{gathered}2\times10\\[2pt]=20\end{gathered},&
\GTenToneHL{cost}{C}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1})&=\begin{gathered}2\times3\\[2pt]=6\end{gathered},&
\GTenToneHL{payoff}{P}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1})&=\begin{gathered}20-6\\[2pt]=14\end{gathered}.
\end{aligned}
\]
All three results are in thousand USD. Lowercase \(r\) and \(c\) name
rates per batch; uppercase \(R\) and \(C\) name monetary totals.

\textbf{Check.} Why is \(20-3\) not the \GTenToneHL{payoff}{profit} for two basic-plan batches?
\textit{Suggested answer:} 20 is total \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue}, but 3 is \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} per batch.
First find total \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost}, \(2\times3=6\), then subtract \(20-6=14\).

\subsubsection*{A revenue rate determined by demand}
Consider a separate pricing variation first: suppose payment comes only
from a demand-based rate, with no plan-based payment. A community booking
scheme pays 5 thousand USD per batch under high demand and 3 under low
demand. Write \(\GTenToneHL{revenue}{r}(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1})=\GTenToneHL{revenue}{5}\) and \(\GTenToneHL{revenue}{r}(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_2})=\GTenToneHL{revenue}{3}\) thousand USD per batch;
read \(\GTenToneHL{revenue}{r}(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1})\) as ``\GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue} rate for state one.'' The rate \(\GTenToneHL{revenue}{r}(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})\)
is determined by the demand state. For this state-only pricing variation,
\[
\GTenToneHL{revenue}{R}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})=\GTenToneHL{quantity}{q}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})\GTenToneHL{revenue}{r}(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j}),\qquad
\GTenToneHL{revenue}{R}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1})=\begin{gathered}2\times5\\[2pt]=10\end{gathered}.
\]
The result is in thousand USD. The rate is the same down a state column,
but total \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue} need not be: three batches under \(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_2}\) earn
\(3\times5=15\) thousand USD in that same state.

Return now to the plan-based pricing version and \emph{extend} it: assume
the booking scheme's payment is an addition to the plan payment, so both
rates apply to each batch. Then
\[
\begin{aligned}
\GTenToneHL{revenue}{R}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})&=\GTenToneHL{quantity}{q}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})\GTenToneHL{revenue}{r}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i})+\GTenToneHL{quantity}{q}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})\GTenToneHL{revenue}{r}(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})\\
          &=\GTenToneHL{quantity}{q}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})[\GTenToneHL{revenue}{r}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i})+\GTenToneHL{revenue}{r}(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})].
\end{aligned}
\]
For the basic plan under high demand, \(2\times10+2\times5=2(10+5)=30\)
thousand USD. Factoring out \GTenToneHL{quantity}{quantity} works because the same two batches
earn both rates. We add 10 and 5 because both use thousand USD per batch;
multiplying the combined rate by two batches gives money.

\subsubsection*{A cost rate determined by demand}
High-demand bookings may use shared deliveries, while low-demand bookings
need separate handling. Suppose handling \GTenToneHL{cost}{costs} 1 thousand USD per batch in
high demand and 2 in low demand. Write \(\GTenToneHL{cost}{c}(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1})=\GTenToneHL{cost}{1}\) and \(\GTenToneHL{cost}{c}(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_2})=\GTenToneHL{cost}{2}\)
thousand USD per batch; read \(\GTenToneHL{cost}{c}(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1})\) as ``\GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} rate for state one.''
The general state-based rate is \(\GTenToneHL{cost}{c}(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})\). In a simple variation where
handling is the only charged \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost},
\[
\GTenToneHL{cost}{C}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})=\GTenToneHL{quantity}{q}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})\GTenToneHL{cost}{c}(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j}),\qquad
\GTenToneHL{cost}{C}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1})=\begin{gathered}2\times1\\[2pt]=2\end{gathered}.
\]
The result is in thousand USD. Now extend the plan-\GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} version instead:
assume handling is charged \emph{in addition} to the plan's resource \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost}.
Then
\[
\GTenToneHL{cost}{C}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})=\GTenToneHL{quantity}{q}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})[\GTenToneHL{cost}{c}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i})+\GTenToneHL{cost}{c}(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})],\qquad
\GTenToneHL{cost}{C}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1})=\begin{gathered}2(3+1)\\[2pt]=8\end{gathered}.
\]
The handling rate stays at 1 down the high-demand column, but its total
charge changes from \(2\times1=2\) to \(3\times1=3\) thousand USD as
\GTenToneHL{quantity}{quantity} changes. A state-based component does not make the entire total
state-only when \GTenToneHL{quantity}{quantity} also depends on the \GTenToneHL{alternative}{alternative}.

\textbf{Check.} Under the version with both \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue} rates and both \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost}
rates, what is the basic plan's high-demand \GTenToneHL{payoff}{profit}?
\textit{Suggested answer:} \GTenToneHL{revenue}{Revenue} is 30, \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} is 8, and \GTenToneHL{payoff}{profit} is
\(30-8=22\) thousand USD. Equal state rates across plans do not imply equal totals.

\subsubsection*{Fixed revenue: first a plan payment, then a state payment}
Extend the version with both \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue} rates: a sponsor now pays 7 thousand
USD for choosing the basic plan, or 9 for choosing the expanded plan,
regardless of how many batches are completed. We write \(\GTenToneHL{revenue}{f_r}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1})=\GTenToneHL{revenue}{7}\) and
\(\GTenToneHL{revenue}{f_r}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_2})=\GTenToneHL{revenue}{9}\) thousand USD. Read \(\GTenToneHL{revenue}{f_r}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1})\) as ``fixed \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue} for
\GTenToneHL{alternative}{alternative} one.'' The letter \(f\) indicates a fixed amount; the subscript
\(r\) identifies it as \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue}. The plan determines \(\GTenToneHL{revenue}{f_r}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i})\).
For the basic plan under high demand, \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue} becomes
\[
\GTenToneHL{revenue}{R}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1})=\begin{gathered}2(10+5)+7\\[2pt]=37\end{gathered}\quad\text{(thousand USD).}
\]
The 7 is added once; it is not a payment for each batch.

Add one more assumption: a community grant pays 6 thousand USD if demand
is high and 7 if it is low, independently of the number of batches served.
Write \(\GTenToneHL{revenue}{f_r}(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1})=\GTenToneHL{revenue}{6}\) and \(\GTenToneHL{revenue}{f_r}(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_2})=\GTenToneHL{revenue}{7}\) thousand USD. Read \(\GTenToneHL{revenue}{f_r}(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1})\)
as ``fixed \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue} for state one.'' The state determines \(\GTenToneHL{revenue}{f_r}(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})\).
The high-demand grant alone contributes 6 thousand USD, whether the plan
serves two or three batches. With both fixed payments now included,
\[
\GTenToneHL{revenue}{R}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1})=\begin{gathered}2(10+5)+7+6\\[2pt]=43\end{gathered}\quad\text{(thousand USD).}
\]
``Fixed'' means independent of \GTenToneHL{quantity}{quantity} within the model. A fixed amount
can still differ between plans or states. For a compact \emph{fixed-only}
variation, suppose there are no per-batch earnings, only the sponsor and
grant. Then \(\GTenToneHL{revenue}{R}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})=\GTenToneHL{revenue}{f_r}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i})+\GTenToneHL{revenue}{f_r}(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})\), and
\(\GTenToneHL{revenue}{R}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1})=\GTenToneHL{revenue}{7}+6=13\) thousand USD; no \GTenToneHL{quantity}{quantity} is needed.

\subsubsection*{Fixed cost: first setup, then administration}
Return to the version with both per-batch \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} rates. Extend it by charging
setup once: 8 thousand USD for the basic plan and 10 for the expanded plan.
Write \(\GTenToneHL{cost}{f_c}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1})=\GTenToneHL{cost}{8}\) and \(\GTenToneHL{cost}{f_c}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_2})=\GTenToneHL{cost}{10}\) thousand USD. Read \(\GTenToneHL{cost}{f_c}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1})\)
as ``fixed \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} for \GTenToneHL{alternative}{alternative} one.'' The subscript \(c\) identifies \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost};
the plan determines \(\GTenToneHL{cost}{f_c}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i})\). For the basic plan under high demand,
\[
\GTenToneHL{cost}{C}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1})=\begin{gathered}2(3+1)+8\\[2pt]=16\end{gathered}\quad\text{(thousand USD).}
\]
Now add a state-specific administration bill: 5 thousand USD under high
demand or 3 under low demand, charged once whichever plan is chosen.
Write \(\GTenToneHL{cost}{f_c}(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1})=\GTenToneHL{cost}{5}\) and \(\GTenToneHL{cost}{f_c}(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_2})=\GTenToneHL{cost}{3}\) thousand USD. Read \(\GTenToneHL{cost}{f_c}(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1})\)
as ``fixed \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} for state one.'' The state determines \(\GTenToneHL{cost}{f_c}(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})\).
The high-demand bill contributes 5 thousand USD, not \(2\times5\).
With setup and administration included,
\[
\GTenToneHL{cost}{C}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1})=\begin{gathered}2(3+1)+8+5\\[2pt]=21\end{gathered}\quad\text{(thousand USD).}
\]
Ordinary positive \GTenToneHL{cost}{costs} are added \emph{inside total \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost}}. Total \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} is
then subtracted once from \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue}: \(43-21=22\) thousand USD. Making every
\GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} negative and then subtracting it would reverse its effect.

\textbf{Check.} If the basic plan completes one batch instead of two, does
its setup charge become 4? \textit{Suggested answer:} No. Setup remains
8 thousand USD; only the per-batch charges change with \GTenToneHL{quantity}{quantity}.

\subsubsection*{Assemble the complete models}
We have now taught every ingredient. When \emph{all} the arrangements just
introduced apply together, their complete models are
\[
\begin{aligned}
\GTenToneHL{revenue}{R}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})&=\GTenToneHL{quantity}{q}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})[\GTenToneHL{revenue}{r}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i})+\GTenToneHL{revenue}{r}(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})]+\GTenToneHL{revenue}{f_r}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i})+\GTenToneHL{revenue}{f_r}(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j}),\\
\GTenToneHL{cost}{C}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})&=\GTenToneHL{quantity}{q}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})[\GTenToneHL{cost}{c}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i})+\GTenToneHL{cost}{c}(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})]+\GTenToneHL{cost}{f_c}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i})+\GTenToneHL{cost}{f_c}(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j}),\\
\GTenToneHL{payoff}{P}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})&=\GTenToneHL{revenue}{R}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})-\GTenToneHL{cost}{C}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j}).
\end{aligned}
\]
The four additive \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue} components are \(\GTenToneHL{quantity}{q}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})\GTenToneHL{revenue}{r}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i})\),
\(\GTenToneHL{quantity}{q}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})\GTenToneHL{revenue}{r}(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})\), \(\GTenToneHL{revenue}{f_r}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i})\), and \(\GTenToneHL{revenue}{f_r}(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})\).
The four additive \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} components are \(\GTenToneHL{quantity}{q}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})\GTenToneHL{cost}{c}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i})\),
\(\GTenToneHL{quantity}{q}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})\GTenToneHL{cost}{c}(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})\), \(\GTenToneHL{cost}{f_c}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i})\), and \(\GTenToneHL{cost}{f_c}(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})\).
Each is a monetary amount. Quantity \(\GTenToneHL{quantity}{q}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})\) is a multiplier,
not an extra monetary component.

The later sections count components \emph{within} the \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue} and \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost}
models. A one-component example can have one \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue} component and one
\GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} component. The two models need not use matching types. For example,
if the stated arrangements provide only plan-based per-batch \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue} and
state-based handling \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost}, then
\[
\GTenToneHL{revenue}{R}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})=\GTenToneHL{quantity}{q}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})\GTenToneHL{revenue}{r}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i}),\qquad
\GTenToneHL{cost}{C}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})=\GTenToneHL{quantity}{q}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})\GTenToneHL{cost}{c}(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j}).
\]
If instead there is only a plan sponsorship payment and a state
administration bill, the fixed-only models are
\[
\GTenToneHL{revenue}{R}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})=\GTenToneHL{revenue}{f_r}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i}),\qquad \GTenToneHL{cost}{C}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})=\GTenToneHL{cost}{f_c}(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j}).
\]
A two-component version might add a fixed plan payment to per-batch plan
\GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue}; a three-component version could add a fixed state grant as well.
Use only components included by the stated arrangements. An excluded
component can be treated as zero. A necessary input that is merely missing
is unknown, not automatically zero.

\subsubsection*{Keep the meaning of a signed adjustment}
Some financial descriptions may explicitly give a reduction. For example,
a 2 thousand USD rebate on a 9 thousand USD bill gives total \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost}
\(9+(-2)=7\), whereas an additional charge of 2 gives \(9+2=11\).
A reduction and a further \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} have opposite effects. If a stated model
actually gives a negative total \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost}, subtract it with parentheses:
\GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue} 5 and total \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} \(-2\) give \GTenToneHL{payoff}{profit} \(5-(-2)=7\) thousand USD.
These signed cases do not change the basic rule: build the stated totals,
then subtract \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} once.

The Advanced General Problem now brings the familiar steps together. Its
community business is a variation of our service enterprise: it uses the
same component values, with one completed service batch counted as one
``unit.'' Its input table connects every \GTenToneHL{quantity}{quantity}, rate, and fixed amount
to the notation above. Rates are in thousand USD per unit, while fixed
amounts and totals are in thousand USD.

	\subsection{Advanced General Problem (C1--C3)}
	\label{sec:problem2-advanced-general-c1-c3}

	A community business must choose the basic operating plan \(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1}\) or expanded operating plan \(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_2}\) before demand is revealed as high (\(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1}\)) or low (\(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_2}\)). Its demand study indicates that plan \(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1}\) will complete \(2\) units in state \(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1}\) and \(1\) in state \(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_2}\), whereas plan \(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_2}\) will complete \(3\) and \(2\) units, respectively. Each completed service batch counts as one unit. \GTenToneHL{revenue}{Revenue} and \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} rates are in thousand USD per unit; fixed amounts and profits are in thousand USD. Each variable rate applies to every unit completed.

	The plans command different base selling rates because they offer different services: each unit under \(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1}\) earns an \GTenToneHL{alternative}{alternative}-based \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue} rate of \(10\), while each unit under \(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_2}\) earns \(11\). Demand conditions add a market-based \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue} rate of \(5\) per unit in \(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1}\) or \(3\) per unit in \(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_2}\). The signed agreements also provide plan-based fixed \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue} of \(7\) for \(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1}\) and \(9\) for \(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_2}\), while community funding adds state-based fixed \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue} of \(6\) in \(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1}\) and \(7\) in \(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_2}\).

	The business's operating budget assigns an \GTenToneHL{alternative}{alternative}-based \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} of \(3\) per unit to \(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1}\) and \(4\) per unit to \(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_2}\), reflecting the resources used by each plan. Demand-related handling adds a further state-based \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} of \(1\) per unit in \(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1}\) and \(2\) per unit in \(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_2}\). Preparing the plans also creates fixed \GTenToneHL{cost}{costs} of \(8\) for \(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1}\) and \(10\) for \(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_2}\), and state-specific administration creates fixed \GTenToneHL{cost}{costs} of \(5\) in \(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1}\) and \(3\) in \(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_2}\). The business must combine all four \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue} components and all four \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} components for each pair, then subtract total \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} from total \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue}.

	\begin{samepage}
	\textbf{Questions/Tasks.}
	\begin{enumerate}
		\CriterionTask
		{subsec:problem2-advanced-general-c1}{1}
		{Interpret the decision elements.}

		\CriterionTask
		{subsec:problem2-advanced-general-c3}{3}
		{Build the payoff table.}
	\end{enumerate}
	\end{samepage}

	\subsubsection*{C1 - Interpreting the decision.}
	\phantomsection
	\label{subsec:problem2-advanced-general-c1}

	\DecisionElementsTable
	{Basic operating plan; Expanded operating plan}
	{High demand; Low demand}
	{Customer bookings reveal demand after the business chooses its plan}
	{Service delivery earns income and uses resources; \GTenToneHL{payoff}{profit} measures the result in thousand USD}

Use the complete models developed above: calculate \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue} and \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} independently,
then subtract \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} from \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue}.

\textbf{Trace one cell before completing the tables.} For \((\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1})\),
use \GTenToneHL{quantity}{quantity} 2, \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue} rates 10 and 5, and fixed \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue} 7 and 6:
\[
\GTenToneHL{revenue}{R}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1})=\begin{gathered}2(10+5)+7+6\\[2pt]=43\end{gathered}.
\]
Independently, use \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} rates 3 and 1 and fixed \GTenToneHL{cost}{costs} 8 and 5:
\[
\GTenToneHL{cost}{C}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1})=\begin{gathered}2(3+1)+8+5\\[2pt]=21\end{gathered}.
\]
Finally, \(\GTenToneHL{payoff}{P}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1})=\GTenToneHL{payoff}{43}-21=22\) thousand USD. This is the entry in row
\(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1}\), column \(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1}\) of the final \GTenToneHL{payoff}{profit} table. Apply these same three
steps to each remaining pair; do not mix a rate or payment from another
plan or state. The following paired tables show \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue} first, \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} second,
and \GTenToneHL{payoff}{profit} third.

	\secInicio[sec:problem2-advanced-general-c1-c3][sec:introduction]

	\begin{CalculationLandscape}{14in}{11in}
\subsubsection*{C3 - Calculating revenue, cost, and payoff.}
	\phantomsection
	\label{subsec:problem2-advanced-general-c3}

\textbf{1. Symbols, values, and meanings.}

\begin{longtable}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.19\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.25\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.50\linewidth}@{}}
\toprule Symbol & Value & Meaning in this problem. \\ \midrule
\endhead
\(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1}\) & --- & Operating plan 1. \\
\(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_2}\) & --- & Operating plan 2. \\
\(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1}\) & --- & Demand outcome 1. \\
\(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_2}\) & --- & Demand outcome 2. \\

\subsection{Reference and classroom solving routine}
\label{subsec:intro-reference}

\textbf{Notation already used.} Monetary units below are thousand USD;
``per unit'' refers to the \GTenToneHL{quantity}{quantity} unit stated in the problem.
\begin{longtable}{@{}p{0.30\linewidth}p{0.64\linewidth}@{}}
\toprule Symbol & Meaning and units \\ \midrule
\endhead
\(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i}\), \(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j}\) & Alternative and state labels; \(i\) and \(j\) are indices, not money or ranks. \\
\(\GTenToneHL{quantity}{q}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})\) & Quantity for that pair, in batches or another stated \GTenToneHL{quantity}{quantity} unit. \\
\(\GTenToneHL{revenue}{r}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i})\), \(\GTenToneHL{revenue}{r}(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})\) & \GTenToneHL{revenue}{Revenue} rates determined by the plan or the state, in thousand USD per unit. \\
\(\GTenToneHL{cost}{c}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i})\), \(\GTenToneHL{cost}{c}(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})\) & \GTenToneHL{cost}{Cost} rates determined by the plan or the state, in thousand USD per unit. \\
\(\GTenToneHL{revenue}{f_r}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i})\), \(\GTenToneHL{revenue}{f_r}(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})\) & Fixed \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue} determined by the plan or the state, in thousand USD; add once. \\
\(\GTenToneHL{cost}{f_c}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i})\), \(\GTenToneHL{cost}{f_c}(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})\) & Fixed \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} determined by the plan or the state, in thousand USD; add once inside \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost}. \\
\(\GTenToneHL{revenue}{R}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})\) & Total \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue} for the pair, in thousand USD. \\
\(\GTenToneHL{cost}{C}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})\) & Total \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} for the pair, in thousand USD. \\
\(\GTenToneHL{payoff}{P}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})\) & Profit/\GTenToneHL{payoff}{payoff} for the pair: \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue} minus \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost}, in thousand USD. \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}

\textbf{Practical solving procedure.}
\begin{enumerate}[itemsep=2pt,parsep=0pt]
\item Identify who chooses, the \GTenToneHL{alternative}{alternatives}, the uncertain process, its
states, the timing, and the consequences in their economic context (C1).
\item Map choices and states to symbols. List all \GTenToneHL{alternative}{alternative}--state pairs.
\item Record inputs in a \emph{Symbol | Value | Meaning in this problem}
table, with units. Distinguish direct totals, quantities, rates, and fixed amounts;
check that necessary inputs are supplied.
\item Calculate \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue} for each pair from its stated components, or record
the directly supplied total. Match \GTenToneHL{quantity}{quantity} units to rate units.
\item Calculate \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} independently for the same pair, or record the supplied
total. Add each applicable fixed amount once.
\item Subtract total \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} from total \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue} (C3). Match the symbolic
calculation to the full numerical substitution and its result.
\item Construct the \GTenToneHL{payoff}{profit} \GTenToneHL{payoff}{payoff} table (C3): \GTenToneHL{alternative}{alternatives} in rows, states
in columns, profits only in cells, and monetary units stated. Keep the same
row and column order throughout.
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Final checks with suggested answers.}
\begin{itemize}[itemsep=2pt,parsep=0pt]
\item Is 10 thousand USD per batch a total \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue}? \textit{No: it is a
rate. Two batches earn 20 thousand USD at that rate.}
\item Does a fixed payment of 7 thousand USD become 14 for two batches?
\textit{No: add it once. Only per-batch amounts are multiplied by \GTenToneHL{quantity}{quantity}.}
\item With \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue} 43 and \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} 21 thousand USD, what enters the \GTenToneHL{payoff}{payoff} table?
\textit{Profit: \(43-21=22\) thousand USD.}
\end{itemize}

\section{Direct Revenue and Cost Models}
	\label{sec:direct-revenue-cost-models}

	\SectionProblemsTableOfContents{
		\SectionProblemLink{sec:problem3-local-cafe-investment-c1-c3}{Local Café Investment (C1--C3)} \\

	\subsection{Local Café Investment (C1--C3)}
	\label{sec:problem3-local-cafe-investment-c1-c3}

	A small café must decide whether to launch a line of artisan desserts or keep its current menu before learning whether daily customer traffic will be high or low. The owner prepared direct total-\GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue} forecasts from expected café sales and direct total-\GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} budgets covering ingredients, staffing, preparation, and other resources. Every amount and the resulting daily \GTenToneHL{payoff}{profit} are in thousand USD.

	Launching the dessert line could generate \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue} of \(58\) when traffic is high, when more customers buy the new products, and \(27\) when traffic is low. The matching operating budgets are \(16\) and \(19\); the low-traffic \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} remains comparatively high because ingredients and preparation must be arranged before actual traffic is known. Keeping the current menu instead is forecast to produce \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue} of \(49\) under high traffic and \(41\) under low traffic, with established-menu operating \GTenToneHL{cost}{costs} of \(21\) in either state. The owner must calculate \GTenToneHL{payoff}{profit} for all four outcomes.

	\textbf{Questions/Tasks.}
	\begin{enumerate}
		\CriterionTask
		{subsec:problem3-local-cafe-investment-c1}{1}
		{Interpreting decision alternatives, events, consequences, and states.}

		\CriterionTask
		{subsec:problem3-local-cafe-investment-c3}{3}
		{Calculating profit and building the payoff table.}
	\end{enumerate}

	\subsubsection*{C1 - Interpreting decision alternatives, events, consequences, and states.}
	\phantomsection
	\label{subsec:problem3-local-cafe-investment-c1}

	\DecisionElementsTable
	{Launch dessert line; Keep current menu}
	{High traffic; Low traffic}
	{Actual foot traffic after the decision}
	{Profit in thousand USD for each \GTenToneHL{alternative}{alternative}--state pair}

	\secInicio[sec:problem3-local-cafe-investment-c1-c3][sec:direct-revenue-cost-models]

	\subsubsection*{C3 - Calculating profit and building the payoff table.}
	\phantomsection
	\label{subsec:problem3-local-cafe-investment-c3}

\textbf{1. Symbols, values, and meanings.}

\begin{longtable}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.19\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.25\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.50\linewidth}@{}}
\toprule Symbol & Value & Meaning in this problem. \\ \midrule
\endhead
\(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1}\) & --- & Launch dessert line. \\
\(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_2}\) & --- & Keep current menu. \\
\(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1}\) & --- & High traffic. \\
\(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_2}\) & --- & Low traffic. \\
\(\GTenToneHL{revenue}{R}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1})\) & 58 thousand USD & Forecast total \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue} for \(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1}\) under \(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1}\). \\
\(\GTenToneHL{cost}{C}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1})\) & 16 thousand USD & Budgeted total \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} for \(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1}\) under \(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1}\). \\
\(\GTenToneHL{revenue}{R}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_2})\) & 27 thousand USD & Forecast total \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue} for \(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1}\) under \(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_2}\). \\
\(\GTenToneHL{cost}{C}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_2})\) & 19 thousand USD & Budgeted total \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} for \(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1}\) under \(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_2}\). \\

	\subsection{Green Energy Production Mix (C1--C3)}
	\label{sec:problem4-green-energy-production-mix-c1-c3}

	A renewable-energy company must build wind turbines, solar farms, or a balanced hybrid system before the year's weather is known. Annual electricity sales and the operating requirements of each generation system have been forecast directly for windy, sunny, and cloudy conditions. All \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue}, \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost}, and \GTenToneHL{payoff}{profit} figures are in thousand USD.

	Wind turbines are expected to earn \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue} of \(132\) in windy weather, \(78\) in sunny weather, and \(62\) in cloudy weather because their electricity output changes with conditions; the corresponding operating and maintenance \GTenToneHL{cost}{costs} are \(42\), \(33\), and \(32\). Solar farms instead are forecast to earn \(77\), \(143\), and \(63\) in the same respective states, with \GTenToneHL{cost}{costs} of \(42\), \(48\), and \(38\), reflecting the resources needed at each output level.

	The hybrid system spreads production across both sources, producing forecast \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue} of \(112\) when it is windy, \(107\) when it is sunny, and \(96\) when it is cloudy. Its corresponding operating \GTenToneHL{cost}{costs} are \(42\), \(42\), and \(36\). Management must subtract the appropriate direct \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} from \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue} for each strategy--weather combination.

	\textbf{Questions/Tasks.}
	\begin{enumerate}
		\CriterionTask
		{subsec:problem4-green-energy-production-mix-c1}{1}
		{Interpreting decision alternatives, events, consequences, and states.}

		\CriterionTask
		{subsec:problem4-green-energy-production-mix-c3}{3}
		{Calculating profit and building the payoff table.}
	\end{enumerate}

	\section{One-Component Models}
	\label{sec:one-component-models}

	\SectionProblemsTableOfContents{
		\SectionProblemLink{sec:problem5-custom-printing-product-line-c1-c3}{Custom Printing Product Line (C1--C3)} \\
		\SectionProblemLink{sec:problem6-event-equipment-fleet-c1-c3}{Event Equipment Fleet (C1--C3)} \\

	\subsection{Custom Printing Product Line (C1--C3)}
	\label{sec:problem5-custom-printing-product-line-c1-c3}

	A custom-printing cooperative must choose to produce premium notebooks or canvas bags before it knows whether the market will bring strong demand with stable material prices or weak demand with higher material prices. Orders already under discussion indicate that premium notebooks will sell \(6\) production lots in the strong state and \(4\) in the weak state, while canvas bags will sell \(5\) and \(3\) lots, respectively.

	Because the two products have different selling prices, each notebook lot earns an \GTenToneHL{alternative}{alternative}-based \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue} rate of \(12\) thousand USD and each canvas-bag lot earns \(15\) thousand USD. Suppliers quote by market condition rather than product: stable prices make the \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} \(5\) thousand USD per lot in the strong state, while higher prices raise it to \(8\) thousand USD per lot in the weak state. These are the only \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue} and \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} components, so the cooperative must apply both rates to the appropriate forecast lot \GTenToneHL{quantity}{quantity}.

	\textbf{Questions/Tasks.}
	\begin{enumerate}
		\CriterionTask
		{subsec:problem5-custom-printing-product-line-c1}{1}
		{Interpreting decision alternatives, events, consequences, and states.}

		\CriterionTask
		{subsec:problem5-custom-printing-product-line-c3}{3}
		{Calculating profit and building the payoff table.}
	\end{enumerate}

	\subsubsection*{C1 - Interpreting decision alternatives, events, consequences, and states.}
	\phantomsection
	\label{subsec:problem5-custom-printing-product-line-c1}

	\DecisionElementsTable
	{Produce premium notebooks; Produce canvas bags}
	{Strong demand and stable material prices; Weak demand and higher material prices}
	{Market conditions revealed after the product-line decision}
	{Profit in thousand USD for each product-line--market-state pair}

	The only \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue} component is an \GTenToneHL{alternative}{alternative}-based variable component:
	\[
	\GTenToneHL{revenue}{R}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})=\GTenToneHL{quantity}{q}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})\GTenToneHL{revenue}{r}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i}).
	\]
	The only \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} component is a state-based variable component:
	\[
	\GTenToneHL{cost}{C}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})=\GTenToneHL{quantity}{q}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})\GTenToneHL{cost}{c}(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j}).
	\]
	Therefore,
	\[
	\GTenToneHL{payoff}{P}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})
	=
	\GTenToneHL{quantity}{q}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})\GTenToneHL{revenue}{r}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i})
	-
	\GTenToneHL{quantity}{q}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})\GTenToneHL{cost}{c}(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j}).
	\]

	\secInicio[sec:problem5-custom-printing-product-line-c1-c3][sec:one-component-models]

	\subsubsection*{C3 - Calculating profit and building the payoff table.}
	\phantomsection
	\label{subsec:problem5-custom-printing-product-line-c3}

\textbf{1. Symbols, values, and meanings.}

\begin{longtable}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.19\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.25\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.50\linewidth}@{}}
\toprule Symbol & Value & Meaning in this problem. \\ \midrule
\endhead
\(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1}\) & --- & Produce premium notebooks. \\
\(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_2}\) & --- & Produce canvas bags. \\
\(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1}\) & --- & Strong demand and stable material prices. \\
\(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_2}\) & --- & Weak demand and higher material prices. \\
\(\GTenToneHL{quantity}{q}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1})\) & 6 production lots & Forecast \GTenToneHL{quantity}{quantity} for \(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1}\) under \(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1}\). \\
\(\GTenToneHL{revenue}{r}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1})\) & 12 thousand USD per production lot & Product selling rate for \(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1}\) (rate per unit). \\
\(\GTenToneHL{cost}{c}(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1})\) & 5 thousand USD per production lot & Material price per lot for \(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1}\) (rate per unit). \\
\(\GTenToneHL{quantity}{q}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_2})\) & 4 production lots & Forecast \GTenToneHL{quantity}{quantity} for \(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1}\) under \(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_2}\). \\
\(\GTenToneHL{cost}{c}(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_2})\) & 8 thousand USD per production lot & Material price per lot for \(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_2}\) (rate per unit). \\

\bottomrule

	\secInicio[sec:problem5-custom-printing-product-line-c1-c3][sec:one-component-models]

	\subsection{Event Equipment Fleet (C1--C3)}
	\label{sec:problem6-event-equipment-fleet-c1-c3}

	An event-equipment provider must prepare either a compact fleet or an expanded fleet before rental demand is known. Booking enquiries indicate that the compact fleet will fulfil \(6\) rental contracts under high demand and \(3\) under low demand; the expanded fleet has capacity for \(8\) and \(4\) contracts in those respective states.

	Market prices make every completed contract earn \(9\) thousand USD when demand is high and \(6\) thousand USD when it is low, regardless of the fleet selected. Preparing, transporting, and inspecting the equipment requires a fixed outlay of \(22\) thousand USD for the compact fleet or \(34\) thousand USD for the expanded fleet. No other \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue} or \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} component is modeled, so the provider must compare state-based contract \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue} with the chosen fleet's fixed preparation \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost}.

	\textbf{Questions/Tasks.}
	\begin{enumerate}
		\CriterionTask
		{subsec:problem6-event-equipment-fleet-c1}{1}
		{Interpreting decision alternatives, events, consequences, and states.}

		\CriterionTask
		{subsec:problem6-event-equipment-fleet-c3}{3}
		{Calculating profit and building the payoff table.}
	\end{enumerate}

	\subsubsection*{C1 - Interpreting decision alternatives, events, consequences, and states.}
	\phantomsection
	\label{subsec:problem6-event-equipment-fleet-c1}

	\DecisionElementsTable
	{Prepare a compact fleet; Prepare an expanded fleet}
	{High rental demand; Low rental demand}
	{Rental demand revealed after the fleet-size decision}
	{Profit in thousand USD for each fleet--demand pair}

	The only \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue} component is a state-based variable component:
	\[
	\GTenToneHL{revenue}{R}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})=\GTenToneHL{quantity}{q}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})\GTenToneHL{revenue}{r}(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j}).
	\]
	The only \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} component is an \GTenToneHL{alternative}{alternative}-based fixed component:
	\[
	\GTenToneHL{cost}{C}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})=\GTenToneHL{cost}{f_c}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i}).
	\]
	Therefore,
	\[
	\GTenToneHL{payoff}{P}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})
	=
	\GTenToneHL{quantity}{q}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})\GTenToneHL{revenue}{r}(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})
	-
	\GTenToneHL{cost}{f_c}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i}).
	\]

	\secInicio[sec:problem6-event-equipment-fleet-c1-c3][sec:one-component-models]

	\secInicio[sec:problem6-event-equipment-fleet-c1-c3][sec:one-component-models]

	\subsection{Community Arts Sponsorship (C1--C3)}
	\label{sec:problem7-community-arts-sponsorship-c1-c3}

	A community arts organisation must accept either a standard or premium sponsorship agreement before officials confirm whether its event faces routine permit requirements or enhanced safety requirements. The sponsor guarantees a fixed payment of \(52\) thousand USD under the standard agreement or \(68\) thousand USD under the premium agreement, so this \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue} depends only on the chosen agreement.

	The authorities' requirements determine the event's fixed regulatory and safety bill: routine permits \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} \(18\) thousand USD, while enhanced safety measures \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} \(31\) thousand USD, whichever sponsorship agreement was selected. Because neither payment changes with attendance or output, no \GTenToneHL{quantity}{quantity} is required; the organisation simply subtracts the relevant state-based fixed \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} from its agreement-based fixed \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue}.

	\textbf{Questions/Tasks.}
	\begin{enumerate}
		\CriterionTask
		{subsec:problem7-community-arts-sponsorship-c1}{1}
		{Interpreting decision alternatives, events, consequences, and states.}

		\CriterionTask
		{subsec:problem7-community-arts-sponsorship-c3}{3}
		{Calculating profit and building the payoff table.}
	\end{enumerate}

	\subsubsection*{C1 - Interpreting decision alternatives, events, consequences, and states.}
	\phantomsection
	\label{subsec:problem7-community-arts-sponsorship-c1}

	\DecisionElementsTable
	{Accept the standard sponsorship agreement; Accept the premium sponsorship agreement}
	{Routine permit requirements; Enhanced safety requirements}
	{Event requirements confirmed after the sponsorship decision}
	{Profit in thousand USD for each sponsorship--requirement pair}

	The only \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue} component is an \GTenToneHL{alternative}{alternative}-based fixed component:
	\[
	\GTenToneHL{revenue}{R}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})=\GTenToneHL{revenue}{f_r}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i}).
	\]
	The only \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} component is a state-based fixed component:
	\[
	\GTenToneHL{cost}{C}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})=\GTenToneHL{cost}{f_c}(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j}).
	\]
	Therefore,
	\[
	\GTenToneHL{payoff}{P}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})
	=
	\GTenToneHL{revenue}{f_r}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i})
	-
	\GTenToneHL{cost}{f_c}(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j}).
	\]

	\secInicio[sec:problem7-community-arts-sponsorship-c1-c3][sec:one-component-models]

	\subsubsection*{C3 - Calculating profit and building the payoff table.}
	\phantomsection
	\label{subsec:problem7-community-arts-sponsorship-c3}

\textbf{1. Symbols, values, and meanings.}

\begin{longtable}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.19\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.25\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.50\linewidth}@{}}
\toprule Symbol & Value & Meaning in this problem. \\ \midrule
\endhead
\(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1}\) & --- & Accept the standard sponsorship agreement. \\
\(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_2}\) & --- & Accept the premium sponsorship agreement. \\
\(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1}\) & --- & Routine permit requirements. \\
\(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_2}\) & --- & Enhanced safety requirements. \\
\(\GTenToneHL{revenue}{f_r}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1})\) & 52 thousand USD & Sponsorship payment for \(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1}\) (fixed amount). \\

	\subsection{Distribution Contract Fulfilment (C1--C3)}
	\label{sec:problem8-distribution-contract-fulfilment-c1-c3}

	A distribution company must choose manual or automated packing before a new contract's volume is confirmed as standard or high. Capacity forecasts show manual packing processing \(4\) hundreds of orders under the standard-volume contract and \(7\) under the high-volume contract, while automated packing processes \(5\) and \(8\) hundreds, respectively.

	The customer pays a fixed amount for the realised contract size: \(70\) thousand USD for standard volume or \(104\) thousand USD for high volume, regardless of the fulfilment method. Labour makes manual processing \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} \(7\) thousand USD per hundred orders, whereas automation lowers that method-based rate to \(5\) thousand USD per hundred orders. With no other components, the company must deduct the chosen method's \GTenToneHL{quantity}{quantity}-based processing \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} from the state's fixed contract payment.

	\textbf{Questions/Tasks.}
	\begin{enumerate}
		\CriterionTask
		{subsec:problem8-distribution-contract-fulfilment-c1}{1}
		{Interpreting decision alternatives, events, consequences, and states.}

		\CriterionTask
		{subsec:problem8-distribution-contract-fulfilment-c3}{3}
		{Calculating profit and building the payoff table.}
	\end{enumerate}

	\subsubsection*{C1 - Interpreting decision alternatives, events, consequences, and states.}
	\phantomsection
	\label{subsec:problem8-distribution-contract-fulfilment-c1}

	\DecisionElementsTable
	{Use manual packing; Use automated packing}
	{Standard-volume contract; High-volume contract}
	{Contract volume revealed after the fulfilment-method decision}
	{Profit in thousand USD for each fulfilment-method--volume pair}

	The only \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue} component is a state-based fixed component:
	\[
	\GTenToneHL{revenue}{R}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})=\GTenToneHL{revenue}{f_r}(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j}).
	\]
	The only \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} component is an \GTenToneHL{alternative}{alternative}-based variable component:
	\[
	\GTenToneHL{cost}{C}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})=\GTenToneHL{quantity}{q}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})\GTenToneHL{cost}{c}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i}).
	\]
	Therefore,
	\[
	\GTenToneHL{payoff}{P}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})
	=
	\GTenToneHL{revenue}{f_r}(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})
	-
	\GTenToneHL{quantity}{q}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})\GTenToneHL{cost}{c}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i}).
	\]

	\subsection{Farm Produce Subscription Plans (C1--C3)}
	\label{sec:problem9-farm-produce-subscription-c1-c3}

	A farm cooperative must offer either a standard or premium produce box before seasonal demand is known to be strong or weak. Its subscription forecast gives the standard box \(7\) hundreds of subscriptions in the strong state and \(4\) in the weak state, and the premium box \(6\) and \(3\) hundreds, respectively.

	The standard box's contents generate a plan-based \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue} rate of \(8\) thousand USD per hundred subscriptions, while the premium contents generate \(11\). Strong market conditions add \(3\) thousand USD per hundred subscriptions to either plan's \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue}; weak conditions add \(1\). Packing inputs \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} \(4\) thousand USD per hundred standard subscriptions or \(6\) per hundred premium subscriptions. Finally, seasonal distribution arrangements create a fixed \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} of \(9\) thousand USD under strong demand and \(13\) under weak demand. The cooperative must combine the two \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue} rates, then deduct both the plan-based variable \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} and the state-based fixed distribution \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost}.

	\textbf{Questions/Tasks.}
	\begin{enumerate}
		\CriterionTask
		{subsec:problem9-farm-produce-subscription-c1}{1}
		{Interpreting decision alternatives, events, consequences, and states.}

		\CriterionTask
		{subsec:problem9-farm-produce-subscription-c3}{3}
		{Calculating profit and building the payoff table.}
	\end{enumerate}

	\subsubsection*{C1 - Interpreting decision alternatives, events, consequences, and states.}
	\phantomsection
	\label{subsec:problem9-farm-produce-subscription-c1}

	\DecisionElementsTable
	{Offer a standard produce box; Offer a premium produce box}
	{Strong seasonal demand; Weak seasonal demand}
	{Seasonal market conditions revealed after the subscription-plan decision}
	{Profit in thousand USD for each plan--market-state pair}

	\GTenToneHL{revenue}{Revenue} contains \GTenToneHL{alternative}{alternative}-based and state-based variable components:
	\[
	\GTenToneHL{revenue}{R}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})
	=
	\GTenToneHL{quantity}{q}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})
	\left[
	\GTenToneHL{revenue}{r}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i})+\GTenToneHL{revenue}{r}(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})
	\right].
	\]

	\GTenToneHL{cost}{Cost} contains an \GTenToneHL{alternative}{alternative}-based variable component and a state-based
	fixed component:
	\[
	\GTenToneHL{cost}{C}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})
	=
	\GTenToneHL{quantity}{q}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})\GTenToneHL{cost}{c}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i})+\GTenToneHL{cost}{f_c}(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j}).
	\]

	Therefore,
	\[
	\GTenToneHL{payoff}{P}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})
	=
	\GTenToneHL{quantity}{q}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})
	\left[
	\GTenToneHL{revenue}{r}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i})+\GTenToneHL{revenue}{r}(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})
	\right]
	-
	\left[
	\GTenToneHL{quantity}{q}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})\GTenToneHL{cost}{c}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i})+\GTenToneHL{cost}{f_c}(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})
	\right].
	\]

	\secInicio[sec:problem9-farm-produce-subscription-c1-c3][sec:two-component-models]

\(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_2}\) & --- & Weak seasonal demand. \\
\(\GTenToneHL{quantity}{q}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1})\) & 7 hundreds of subscriptions & Forecast \GTenToneHL{quantity}{quantity} for \(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1}\) under \(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1}\). \\
\(\GTenToneHL{revenue}{r}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1})\) & 8 thousand USD per hundred subscriptions & Produce-box selling rate for \(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1}\) (rate per unit). \\
\(\GTenToneHL{revenue}{r}(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1})\) & 3 thousand USD per hundred subscriptions & Seasonal market supplement for \(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1}\) (rate per unit). \\
\(\GTenToneHL{cost}{c}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1})\) & 4 thousand USD per hundred subscriptions & Packing input rate for \(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1}\) (rate per unit). \\
\(\GTenToneHL{cost}{f_c}(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1})\) & 9 thousand USD & Seasonal distribution \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} for \(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1}\) (fixed amount). \\
\(\GTenToneHL{quantity}{q}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_2})\) & 4 hundreds of subscriptions & Forecast \GTenToneHL{quantity}{quantity} for \(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_1}\) under \(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_2}\). \\
\(\GTenToneHL{revenue}{r}(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_2})\) & 1 thousand USD per hundred subscriptions & Seasonal market supplement for \(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_2}\) (rate per unit). \\
\(\GTenToneHL{cost}{f_c}(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_2})\) & 13 thousand USD & Seasonal distribution \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} for \(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_2}\) (fixed amount). \\
\(\GTenToneHL{quantity}{q}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_2},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1})\) & 6 hundreds of subscriptions & Forecast \GTenToneHL{quantity}{quantity} for \(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_2}\) under \(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_1}\). \\
\(\GTenToneHL{revenue}{r}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_2})\) & 11 thousand USD per hundred subscriptions & Produce-box selling rate for \(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_2}\) (rate per unit). \\
\(\GTenToneHL{cost}{c}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_2})\) & 6 thousand USD per hundred subscriptions & Packing input rate for \(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_2}\) (rate per unit). \\
\(\GTenToneHL{quantity}{q}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_2},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_2})\) & 3 hundreds of subscriptions & Forecast \GTenToneHL{quantity}{quantity} for \(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_2}\) under \(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_2}\). \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}

\CalculationPair{\GTenToneHL{revenue}{Revenue}}{
\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}
\toprule

	\subsection{Mobile Catering Configuration (C1--C3)}
	\label{sec:problem10-mobile-catering-configuration-c1-c3}

	A mobile-catering company must prepare a compact catering station or a full mobile kitchen before learning whether the event will be busy with stable ingredient prices or quiet with higher ingredient prices. Booking forecasts indicate \(8\) hundreds of service orders for the compact station in the busy state and \(4\) in the quiet state, compared with \(10\) and \(5\) hundreds for the full kitchen.

	Menu sales earn a configuration-based \(7\) thousand USD per hundred orders from the compact station or \(9\) from the full kitchen. Booking agreements add fixed \GTenToneHL{revenue}{revenue} of \(18\) thousand USD for the compact configuration and \(25\) for the full configuration. Ingredients \GTenToneHL{cost}{cost} \(3\) thousand USD per hundred orders when prices are stable and \(5\) when prices are higher. In addition, setting up the compact station \GTenToneHL{cost}{costs} a fixed \(20\) thousand USD, while preparing the full kitchen \GTenToneHL{cost}{costs} \(32\). The company must retain each component's \GTenToneHL{alternative}{alternative}- or state-based role when calculating \GTenToneHL{payoff}{profit}.

	\textbf{Questions/Tasks.}
	\begin{enumerate}
		\CriterionTask
		{subsec:problem10-mobile-catering-configuration-c1}{1}
		{Interpreting decision alternatives, events, consequences, and states.}

		\CriterionTask
		{subsec:problem10-mobile-catering-configuration-c3}{3}
		{Calculating profit and building the payoff table.}
	\end{enumerate}

	\subsubsection*{C1 - Interpreting decision alternatives, events, consequences, and states.}
	\phantomsection
	\label{subsec:problem10-mobile-catering-configuration-c1}

	\DecisionElementsTable
	{Prepare a compact catering station; Prepare a full mobile kitchen}
	{Busy event with stable ingredient prices; Quiet event with higher ingredient prices}
	{Event demand and ingredient-market conditions revealed after the configuration decision}
	{Profit in thousand USD for each configuration--market-state pair}

	\GTenToneHL{revenue}{Revenue} contains \GTenToneHL{alternative}{alternative}-based variable and fixed components:
	\[
	\GTenToneHL{revenue}{R}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})
	=
	\GTenToneHL{quantity}{q}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})\GTenToneHL{revenue}{r}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i})+\GTenToneHL{revenue}{f_r}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i}).
	\]

	\GTenToneHL{cost}{Cost} contains a state-based variable component and an \GTenToneHL{alternative}{alternative}-based
	fixed component:
	\[
	\GTenToneHL{cost}{C}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})
	=
	\GTenToneHL{quantity}{q}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})\GTenToneHL{cost}{c}(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})+\GTenToneHL{cost}{f_c}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i}).
	\]

	Therefore,
	\[
	\GTenToneHL{payoff}{P}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})
	=
	\left[
	\GTenToneHL{quantity}{q}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})\GTenToneHL{revenue}{r}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i})+\GTenToneHL{revenue}{f_r}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i})
	\right]
	-
	\left[
	\GTenToneHL{quantity}{q}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})\GTenToneHL{cost}{c}(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})+\GTenToneHL{cost}{f_c}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i})
	\right].
	\]

	\subsection{Online Training Programme (C1--C3)}
	\label{sec:problem11-online-training-programme-c1-c3}

	An online-training provider must offer its foundation or advanced programme before enrolment is known to come from corporate clients or individuals. Past enquiries imply \(5\) enrolment groups for the foundation programme under corporate enrolment and \(3\) under individual enrolment; the advanced programme expects \(4\) and \(2\) groups, respectively.

	Tuition generates \(14\) thousand USD per foundation group or \(20\) thousand USD per advanced group. The enrolment channel also provides a fixed payment of \(12\) thousand USD for corporate enrolment or \(7\) for individual enrolment. Delivering a group \GTenToneHL{cost}{costs} \(6\) thousand USD in the corporate state and \(8\) in the individual state, while state-specific platform arrangements add fixed \GTenToneHL{cost}{costs} of \(10\) and \(14\), respectively. The provider must apply both state-based \GTenToneHL{cost}{costs} to the forecast groups while keeping the fixed amounts independent of \GTenToneHL{quantity}{quantity}.

	\textbf{Questions/Tasks.}
	\begin{enumerate}
		\CriterionTask
		{subsec:problem11-online-training-programme-c1}{1}
		{Interpreting decision alternatives, events, consequences, and states.}

		\CriterionTask
		{subsec:problem11-online-training-programme-c3}{3}
		{Calculating profit and building the payoff table.}
	\end{enumerate}

	\subsubsection*{C1 - Interpreting decision alternatives, events, consequences, and states.}
	\phantomsection
	\label{subsec:problem11-online-training-programme-c1}

	\DecisionElementsTable
	{Offer the foundation programme; Offer the advanced programme}
	{Corporate enrolment; Individual enrolment}
	{Enrolment type revealed after the programme decision}
	{Profit in thousand USD for each programme--enrolment pair}

	\GTenToneHL{revenue}{Revenue} contains an \GTenToneHL{alternative}{alternative}-based variable component and a state-based
	fixed component:
	\[
	\GTenToneHL{revenue}{R}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})
	=
	\GTenToneHL{quantity}{q}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})\GTenToneHL{revenue}{r}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i})+\GTenToneHL{revenue}{f_r}(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j}).
	\]

	\GTenToneHL{cost}{Cost} contains state-based variable and fixed components:
	\[
	\GTenToneHL{cost}{C}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})
	=
	\GTenToneHL{quantity}{q}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})\GTenToneHL{cost}{c}(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})+\GTenToneHL{cost}{f_c}(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j}).
	\]

	Therefore,
	\[
	\GTenToneHL{payoff}{P}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})
	=
	\left[
	\GTenToneHL{quantity}{q}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})\GTenToneHL{revenue}{r}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i})+\GTenToneHL{revenue}{f_r}(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})
	\right]
	-
	\left[
	\GTenToneHL{quantity}{q}(\GTenToneHL{alternative}{A_i},\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})\GTenToneHL{cost}{c}(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})+\GTenToneHL{cost}{f_c}(\GTenToneHL{state}{S_j})
	\right].
	\]
